# Research: subset-b-000616

Grouped research for the listed ceph-client Devicetree SPI, SPMI, SRAM, and thermal binding schemas. Each section preserves the source path and is wrapped for deterministic reconciliation into source-tree-aligned per-file reports.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/spi/ibm,spi-fsi.yaml

**Purpose:** Defines the JSON-schema contract for `IBM FSI-attached SPI Controller` as a SPI bus/controller/peripheral Devicetree binding. It constrains DTS/DTB ABI shape for this hardware class, including stable `compatible` strings, resource properties, provider cells, and any child-node layout used by Linux OF matching and subsystem registration. Source description: A SPI controller found on IBM Power processors, accessed over FSI from a service processor. This node will always be a child node of an ibm,fsi2spi node.

**Important APIs/types/functions:** This file is declarative schema, not executable code. Its public ABI surface is `$id` `http://devicetree.org/schemas/spi/ibm,spi-fsi.yaml#`, `$schema` `http://devicetree.org/meta-schemas/core.yaml#`, `title`, `maintainers`, `properties`, `required`, schema composition keywords, and referenced common bindings. Compatible strings and compatible-pattern values include `ibm,spi-fsi`. Required top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`. Important top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`. Direct and nested `$ref` dependencies are `spi-controller.yaml#`. Property roles include `compatible` for driver match key and stable hardware ABI identifier; `reg` for MMIO/register windows or bus-local address ranges. Top-level closure policy: unevaluatedProperties: False. Maintainers: `Eddie James <eajames@linux.ibm.com>`. The source was read as 56 lines / 1140 bytes.

**Control flow:** Control flow is supplied by dt-schema tooling and by the kernel subsystem that consumes the resulting DTB. `dt_binding_check` loads the core meta-schema, resolves `$ref` entries, applies property schemas, required lists, enum/const/items limits, dependency rules, and composition keywords, then validates the examples. At boot, a node satisfying this schema is selected by OF `compatible` matching; kernel resource helpers parse resources such as `reg`; the relevant subsystem then registers buses, providers, domains, pools, or sensor devices. Schema composition/conditional keywords present: `allOf`. It does not define pattern child-node schemas at top level.

**State and persistence behavior:** The YAML owns no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data. Its persistent effect is ABI stability: DTS files and compiled DTBs that validate against it encode hardware identity, register layout, interrupt or thermal provider cells, bus child addressing, memory partitions, and calibration/resource links for long-lived board descriptions. Changing required fields, compatible sequences, cell counts, or closure policy can break old DTBs even though this source file itself has no runtime storage.

**Dependencies and integration points:** Linux SPI controller, memory-controller, or bridge drivers match `compatible`, map `reg`, acquire `clocks`, `resets`, `dmas`, `interrupts`, GPIO chip selects, and create SPI child devices from bus child nodes. Common integration is with `spi-controller.yaml` and `spi-peripheral-props.yaml`; child `reg` values select chip-selects, while `spi-max-frequency`, polarity, phase, bus width, and delay properties are consumed by the SPI core and individual peripheral drivers. Schema-level dependencies are the core Devicetree meta-schema plus `spi-controller.yaml#`.

**Risks:** Main risks are Devicetree ABI drift, incomplete compatible coverage, stale examples, wrong required-property sets, mismatched register-window order, and conditionals that are either too broad or too strict for existing board DTS files. SPI-specific risk centers on chip-select numbering, `cs-gpios` polarity versus `spi-cs-high`, DMA channel naming, FIFO-depth constraints, bus-width limits, and child peripheral properties that may validate but still fail probe or corrupt transfers when they do not match controller capability. Because these schemas are part of the DT ABI, tightening closure or changing compatible ordering should be treated as a compatibility-sensitive change.

**Test signals:** Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/spi/ibm,spi-fsi.yaml` and `make dtbs_check` for DTS users of every compatible listed here. Verify `$ref` targets resolve, inline examples compile, required-property failures are caught, and compatible-specific `if`/`then` branches are exercised.  Runtime signals include driver probe logs, SPI device enumeration, chip-select polarity tests, PIO/DMA transfer tests, and flash/peripheral read-write smoke tests where applicable. The file contains 1 example block(s), which are part of the schema validation signal.
<!-- END_FILE_RESEARCH: sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/spi/ibm,spi-fsi.yaml -->

Required top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupts`, `interrupt-names`, `clocks`, `clock-names`, `power-domains`, `#address-cells`, `#size-cells`. Important top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupts`, `interrupt-names`, `clocks`, `clock-names`, `resets`, `reset-names`, `dmas`, `dma-names`, `power-domains`. Direct and nested `$ref` dependencies are `spi-controller.yaml#`. Property roles include `compatible` for driver match key and stable hardware ABI identifier; `reg` for MMIO/register windows or bus-local address ranges; `interrupts` for wired interrupt inputs or outputs consumed by the driver; `interrupt-names` for semantic names for interrupt entries; `clocks` for clock provider phandles needed before accessing hardware; `clock-names` for clock name/order ABI for driver lookup; `resets` for reset-controller phandles for hardware initialization; `reset-names` for reset name/order ABI for driver lookup; `dmas` for DMA channel phandles used for transfer offload; `dma-names` for DMA channel names used by subsystem drivers; `power-domains` for power-domain dependencies. Top-level closure policy: unevaluatedProperties: False. Maintainers: `Fabrizio Castro <fabrizio.castro.jz@renesas.com>`. The source was read as 194 lines / 4084 bytes.

**Control flow:** Control flow is supplied by dt-schema tooling and by the kernel subsystem that consumes the resulting DTB. `dt_binding_check` loads the core meta-schema, resolves `$ref` entries, applies property schemas, required lists, enum/const/items limits, dependency rules, and composition keywords, then validates the examples. At boot, a node satisfying this schema is selected by OF `compatible` matching; kernel resource helpers parse resources such as `reg`, `interrupts`, `interrupt-names`, `clocks`, `clock-names`, `resets`, `dmas`, `dma-names`, `power-domains`; the relevant subsystem then registers buses, providers, domains, pools, or sensor devices. Its persistent effect is ABI stability: DTS files and compiled DTBs that validate against it encode hardware identity, register layout, interrupt or thermal provider cells, bus child addressing, memory partitions, and calibration/resource links for long-lived board descriptions. Changing required fields, compatible sequences, cell counts, or closure policy can break old DTBs even though this source file itself has no runtime storage.

**Risks:** Main risks are Devicetree ABI drift, incomplete compatible coverage, stale examples, wrong required-property sets, mismatched register-window order, and conditionals that are either too broad or too strict for existing board DTS files. SPI-specific risk centers on chip-select numbering, `cs-gpios` polarity versus `spi-cs-high`, DMA channel naming, FIFO-depth constraints, bus-width limits, and child peripheral properties that may validate but still fail probe or corrupt transfers when they do not match controller capability. It constrains DTS/DTB ABI shape for this hardware class, including stable `compatible` strings, resource properties, provider cells, and any child-node layout used by Linux OF matching and subsystem registration. Source description: SPI busses can be described with a node for the SPI controller device and a set of child nodes for each SPI slave on the bus. The system SPI controller may be described for use in SPI master mode or in SPI slave mode, but not for both at the same time.

**Important APIs/types/functions:** This file is declarative schema, not executable code. Its public ABI surface is `$id` `http://devicetree.org/schemas/spi/spi-controller.yaml#`, `$schema` `http://devicetree.org/meta-schemas/core.yaml#`, `title`, `maintainers`, `properties`, `required`, schema composition keywords, and referenced common bindings. Compatible strings and compatible-pattern values include none declared. Required top-level properties are none declared. Important top-level properties are `$nodename`, `#address-cells`, `#size-cells`, `cs-gpios`, `fifo-depth`, `rx-fifo-depth`, `tx-fifo-depth`, `num-cs`, `spi-slave`, `slave`. Direct and nested `$ref` dependencies are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/flag`, `spi-peripheral-props.yaml`. Property roles include `#address-cells` for child bus address cell width; `#size-cells` for child bus size cell width; `cs-gpios` for GPIO-backed SPI chip selects and polarity mapping; `num-cs` for number of SPI chip selects; `spi-slave` for selects SPI controller slave mode. Top-level closure policy: additionalProperties: True. Maintainers: `Mark Brown <broonie@kernel.org>`. The source was read as 217 lines / 5864 bytes.

**Control flow:** Control flow is supplied by dt-schema tooling and by the kernel subsystem that consumes the resulting DTB. `dt_binding_check` loads the core meta-schema, resolves `$ref` entries, applies property schemas, required lists, enum/const/items limits, dependency rules, and composition keywords, then validates the examples. At boot, a node satisfying this schema is selected by OF `compatible` matching; kernel resource helpers parse resources such as none declared; the relevant subsystem then registers buses, providers, domains, pools, or sensor devices. Schema composition/conditional keywords present: `allOf`, `dependencies`. Pattern child-node schemas are keyed by `@[0-9a-f]+$`.

**State and persistence behavior:** The YAML owns no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data. Its persistent effect is ABI stability: DTS files and compiled DTBs that validate against it encode hardware identity, register layout, interrupt or thermal provider cells, bus child addressing, memory partitions, and calibration/resource links for long-lived board descriptions. Changing required fields, compatible sequences, cell counts, or closure policy can break old DTBs even though this source file itself has no runtime storage.

**Dependencies and integration points:** Linux SPI controller, memory-controller, or bridge drivers match `compatible`, map `reg`, acquire `clocks`, `resets`, `dmas`, `interrupts`, GPIO chip selects, and create SPI child devices from bus child nodes. Common integration is with `spi-controller.yaml` and `spi-peripheral-props.yaml`; child `reg` values select chip-selects, while `spi-max-frequency`, polarity, phase, bus width, and delay properties are consumed by the SPI core and individual peripheral drivers. Schema-level dependencies are the core Devicetree meta-schema plus `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/flag`, `spi-peripheral-props.yaml`.

**Risks:** Main risks are Devicetree ABI drift, incomplete compatible coverage, stale examples, wrong required-property sets, mismatched register-window order, and conditionals that are either too broad or too strict for existing board DTS files. SPI-specific risk centers on chip-select numbering, `cs-gpios` polarity versus `spi-cs-high`, DMA channel naming, FIFO-depth constraints, bus-width limits, and child peripheral properties that may validate but still fail probe or corrupt transfers when they do not match controller capability. Because these schemas are part of the DT ABI, tightening closure or changing compatible ordering should be treated as a compatibility-sensitive change.

**Test signals:** Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/spi/spi-controller.yaml` and `make dtbs_check` for DTS users of every compatible listed here. Verify `$ref` targets resolve, inline examples compile, required-property failures are caught, and compatible-specific `if`/`then` branches are exercised.  Runtime signals include driver probe logs, SPI device enumeration, chip-select polarity tests, PIO/DMA transfer tests, and flash/peripheral read-write smoke tests where applicable. The file contains 5 example block(s), which are part of the schema validation signal.

It constrains DTS/DTB ABI shape for this hardware class, including stable `compatible` strings, resource properties, provider cells, and any child-node layout used by Linux OF matching and subsystem registration. Source description: This binding describes a SPI bus multiplexer to route the SPI chip select signals. This can be used when you need more devices than the SPI controller has chip selects available. An example setup is shown in ASCII art; the actual setting of the multiplexer to a channel needs to be done by a specific SPI mux driver. MOSI /--------------------------------+--------+--------+--------\ MISO |/------------------------------+|-------+|-------+|-------\| SCL ||/----------------------------+||------+||------+||------\||...

SPI-specific risk centers on chip-select numbering, `cs-gpios` polarity versus `spi-cs-high`, DMA channel naming, FIFO-depth constraints, bus-width limits, and child peripheral properties that may validate but still fail probe or corrupt transfers when they do not match controller capability. Because these schemas are part of the DT ABI, tightening closure or changing compatible ordering should be treated as a compatibility-sensitive change.

Property roles include `compatible` for OF driver matching, `reg` for the controller MMIO window, `clocks` for register and transfer timing, `interrupts` for controller IRQ delivery, `resets` for hardware initialization, `dmas`/`dma-names` for RX/TX and optional RX memory-to-memory offload, `sram` for temporary DMA/MDMA storage, and `access-controllers`/`power-domains` for platform security and power integration. Top-level closure policy is `unevaluatedProperties: false`. Maintainers are `Erwan Leray <erwan.leray@foss.st.com>` and `Fabrice Gasnier <fabrice.gasnier@foss.st.com>`. The source was read as 133 lines / 3035 bytes.

**Control flow:** Control flow is supplied by dt-schema tooling and by the Linux SPI subsystem that consumes the resulting DTB. `dt_binding_check` resolves `spi-controller.yaml#`, applies the STM32-specific property constraints, validates required resources, and evaluates the `allOf` conditionals. For `st,stm32f4-spi`, the schema forbids `st,spi-midi-ns` and `sram`, and limits DMA to RX/TX channels named `rx` and `tx`. For `st,stm32mp25-spi`, it also forbids `sram` and limits DMA to RX/TX. Other variants may expose a third `rxm2m` DMA channel and the `sram` phandle used as temporary staging storage. At boot, the SPI driver matches `compatible`, maps `reg`, enables clocks and optional reset/power/security resources, configures IRQ and DMA channels, and registers a SPI controller whose child nodes are created using inherited SPI-controller bus rules.

**State and persistence behavior:** The YAML owns no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data. Its persistent effect is ABI stability: board DTS files and compiled DTBs record the STM32 SPI variant, MMIO window, interrupt, clock, reset, DMA, SRAM, and child-device layout expected by the kernel. Tightening DMA item counts, changing compatible membership, forbidding or requiring `sram`, or altering inherited SPI child-node semantics can break old board descriptions even though the schema itself has no runtime storage.

**Dependencies and integration points:** Integration is with the generic SPI controller binding, the Linux STM32 SPI driver, common clock/reset/interrupt/DMA frameworks, optional SRAM partitions described by `Documentation/devicetree/bindings/sram/sram.yaml`, power domains, and access-controller policy. Child SPI peripherals depend on inherited SPI bus semantics such as `#address-cells`, `#size-cells`, child `reg` chip-select values, `cs-gpios`, `spi-max-frequency`, and mode flags. The schema-level dependencies are the core Devicetree meta-schema, `spi-controller.yaml#`, and `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/phandle`.

**Risks:** Main risks are Devicetree ABI drift, incomplete compatible coverage, stale examples, wrong required-property sets, and overly broad or strict variant conditionals. STM32-specific risks include accepting an invalid three-channel DMA layout for variants that only support RX/TX, rejecting the SRAM staging phandle on variants that need RX memory-to-memory offload, mismatched `dma-names`, missing reset or power-domain data on platforms that need it, and inherited SPI chip-select polarity mistakes that validate but produce broken transfers. Because the file closes unevaluated properties, adding new platform integration properties requires deliberate schema updates.

**Test signals:** Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/spi/st,stm32-spi.yaml` and `make dtbs_check` for DTS users of each STM32 SPI compatible. Verify the inline example compiles, `st,stm32f4-spi` and `st,stm32mp25-spi` reject forbidden `sram` and third DMA entries, other variants accept the documented RX/TX/RXM2M pattern when needed, and inherited SPI-controller child-device checks still pass. Runtime signals include successful STM32 SPI probe, clock/reset acquisition, IRQ registration, PIO and DMA transfer tests, chip-select polarity checks, and read/write smoke tests against attached SPI peripherals.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/spmi/mediatek,mt8196-spmi.yaml

**Purpose:** Defines the JSON-schema contract for `MediaTek MT8196 SPMI 2.0 Controller` as a SPMI bus/controller Devicetree binding. It constrains DTS/DTB ABI shape for this hardware class, including stable `compatible` strings, resource properties, provider cells, and any child-node layout used by Linux OF matching and subsystem registration. Source description: The MediaTek MT8196 SoC features a SPMI version 2.0 compliant controller, with internal wrapping arbitration logic to allow for multiple on-chip devices to control up to two SPMI buses. The main arbiter also acts as an interrupt controller, arbitering also the interrupts coming from SPMI-connected devices into each of the nested interrupt controllers from any of the present SPMI buses.

Important top-level properties are `compatible`, `ranges`, `#address-cells`, `#size-cells`. Direct and nested `$ref` dependencies are `/schemas/spmi/spmi.yaml`. Property roles include `compatible` for driver match key and stable hardware ABI identifier; `ranges` for child-to-parent address translation; `#address-cells` for child bus address cell width; `#size-cells` for child bus size cell width. Top-level closure policy: additionalProperties: False. Maintainers: `Hsin-Hsiung Wang <hsin-hsiung.wang@mediatek.com>`, `AngeloGioacchino Del Regno <angelogioacchino.delregno@collabora.com>`. **State and persistence behavior:** The YAML owns no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data. Its persistent effect is ABI stability: DTS files and compiled DTBs that validate against it encode hardware identity, register layout, interrupt or thermal provider cells, bus child addressing, memory partitions, and calibration/resource links for long-lived board descriptions. Changing required fields, compatible sequences, cell counts, or closure policy can break old DTBs even though this source file itself has no runtime storage.

Schema-level dependencies are the core Devicetree meta-schema plus `/schemas/spmi/spmi.yaml`.

**Risks:** Main risks are Devicetree ABI drift, incomplete compatible coverage, stale examples, wrong required-property sets, mismatched register-window order, and conditionals that are either too broad or too strict for existing board DTS files. SPMI-specific risk centers on PMIC arbiter register-window ordering, execution-environment/channel selection, interrupt cell widths, child address encoding, and deprecated bus identifiers, any of which can break PMIC IRQ routing or peripheral enumeration. Because these schemas are part of the DT ABI, tightening closure or changing compatible ordering should be treated as a compatibility-sensitive change.

**Purpose:** Defines the JSON-schema contract for `System Power Management Interface (SPMI) Controller` as a SPMI bus/controller Devicetree binding. It constrains DTS/DTB ABI shape for this hardware class, including stable `compatible` strings, resource properties, provider cells, and any child-node layout used by Linux OF matching and subsystem registration. Source description: The System Power Management (SPMI) controller is a 2-wire bus defined by the MIPI Alliance for power management control to be used on SoC designs. SPMI controllers are modelled in device tree using a generic set of bindings defined here, plus any bus controller specific properties, if needed. Each SPMI controller has zero or more child nodes (up to 16 ones), each one representing an unique slave at the bus.

**Important APIs/types/functions:** This file is declarative schema, not executable code. Its public ABI surface is `$id` `http://devicetree.org/schemas/spmi/spmi.yaml#`, `$schema` `http://devicetree.org/meta-schemas/core.yaml#`, `title`, `maintainers`, `properties`, `required`, schema composition keywords, and referenced common bindings. Compatible strings and compatible-pattern values include none declared. Required top-level properties are `reg`. Important top-level properties are `$nodename`, `#address-cells`, `#size-cells`. Direct and nested `$ref` dependencies are none declared. Property roles include `#address-cells` for child bus address cell width; `#size-cells` for child bus size cell width. Top-level closure policy: additionalProperties: True. Maintainers: `Stephen Boyd <sboyd@kernel.org>`. The source was read as 76 lines / 1549 bytes.

**Control flow:** Control flow is supplied by dt-schema tooling and by the kernel subsystem that consumes the resulting DTB. `dt_binding_check` loads the core meta-schema, resolves `$ref` entries, applies property schemas, required lists, enum/const/items limits, dependency rules, and composition keywords, then validates the examples. At boot, a node satisfying this schema is selected by OF `compatible` matching; kernel resource helpers parse resources such as none declared; the relevant subsystem then registers buses, providers, domains, pools, or sensor devices. Schema composition/conditional keywords present: none declared. Pattern child-node schemas are keyed by `@[0-9a-f]$`.

**State and persistence behavior:** The YAML owns no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data. Its persistent effect is ABI stability: DTS files and compiled DTBs that validate against it encode hardware identity, register layout, interrupt or thermal provider cells, bus child addressing, memory partitions, and calibration/resource links for long-lived board descriptions. Changing required fields, compatible sequences, cell counts, or closure policy can break old DTBs even though this source file itself has no runtime storage.

**Dependencies and integration points:** Linux SPMI controller and PMIC-arbiter drivers match `compatible`, map register windows, register SPMI bus providers, and may also register interrupt-controller domains for PMIC peripheral IRQs. Client integration is through SPMI child nodes addressed by `#address-cells`/`#size-cells`, interrupt specifiers, and common PMIC arbiter properties such as execution environment, channel, bus-id, and register-window names. Schema-level dependencies are the core Devicetree meta-schema plus none declared.

**Risks:** Main risks are Devicetree ABI drift, incomplete compatible coverage, stale examples, wrong required-property sets, mismatched register-window order, and conditionals that are either too broad or too strict for existing board DTS files. SPMI-specific risk centers on PMIC arbiter register-window ordering, execution-environment/channel selection, interrupt cell widths, child address encoding, and deprecated bus identifiers, any of which can break PMIC IRQ routing or peripheral enumeration. Because these schemas are part of the DT ABI, tightening closure or changing compatible ordering should be treated as a compatibility-sensitive change.

**Test signals:** Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/spmi/spmi.yaml` and `make dtbs_check` for DTS users of every compatible listed here. Verify `$ref` targets resolve, inline examples compile, required-property failures are caught, and compatible-specific `if`/`then` branches are exercised.  Runtime signals include SPMI bus registration, PMIC child probing, PMIC IRQ delivery, and arbiter register-window access on each supported hardware generation. The file contains 1 example block(s), which are part of the schema validation signal.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/thermal/nvidia,tegra30-tsensor.yaml

**Purpose:** Defines the JSON-schema contract for `NVIDIA Tegra30 Thermal Sensor` as a thermal sensor/controller Devicetree binding. It constrains DTS/DTB ABI shape for this hardware class, including stable `compatible` strings, resource properties, provider cells, and any child-node layout used by Linux OF matching and subsystem registration. Source description: TSENSOR provides thermal and voltage sensors which monitor temperature and voltage of the chip. Sensors are placed across the die to gauge the temperature of the whole chip. The TSENSOR module: Generates an interrupt to SW to lower temperature via DVFS on reaching a certain thermal/voltage threshold. Generates a signal to the CAR to reduce CPU frequency by half on reaching a certain thermal/voltage threshold. Generates a signal to the PMC when the temperature reaches dangerously high levels to reset the chip and...

Source description: Limits Management Hardware(LMh) is a hardware infrastructure on some Qualcomm SoCs that can enforce temperature and current limits as programmed by software for certain IPs like CPU.

**Important APIs/types/functions:** This file is declarative schema, not executable code. Its public ABI surface is `$id` `http://devicetree.org/schemas/thermal/qcom-lmh.yaml#`, `$schema` `http://devicetree.org/meta-schemas/core.yaml#`, `title`, `maintainers`, `properties`, `required`, schema composition keywords, and referenced common bindings. Compatible strings and compatible-pattern values include `qcom,sc8180x-lmh`, `qcom,sdm845-lmh`, `qcom,sm8150-lmh`, `qcom,sdm670-lmh`, `qcom,qcm2290-lmh`. Required top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupts`, `#interrupt-cells`, `interrupt-controller`, `cpus`, `qcom,lmh-temp-arm-millicelsius`, `qcom,lmh-temp-low-millicelsius`, `qcom,lmh-temp-high-millicelsius`. Important top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupts`, `#interrupt-cells`, `interrupt-controller`, `cpus`, `qcom,lmh-temp-arm-millicelsius`, `qcom,lmh-temp-low-millicelsius`, `qcom,lmh-temp-high-millicelsius`. Direct and nested `$ref` dependencies are none declared. Property roles include `compatible` for driver match key and stable hardware ABI identifier; `reg` for MMIO/register windows or bus-local address ranges; `interrupts` for wired interrupt inputs or outputs consumed by the driver; `#interrupt-cells` for interrupt specifier cell width for child/client IRQs. **Control flow:** Control flow is supplied by dt-schema tooling and by the kernel subsystem that consumes the resulting DTB. `dt_binding_check` loads the core meta-schema, resolves `$ref` entries, applies property schemas, required lists, enum/const/items limits, dependency rules, and composition keywords, then validates the examples. At boot, a node satisfying this schema is selected by OF `compatible` matching; kernel resource helpers parse resources such as `reg`, `interrupts`; the relevant subsystem then registers buses, providers, domains, pools, or sensor devices. **State and persistence behavior:** The YAML owns no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data. Its persistent effect is ABI stability: DTS files and compiled DTBs that validate against it encode hardware identity, register layout, interrupt or thermal provider cells, bus child addressing, memory partitions, and calibration/resource links for long-lived board descriptions. Changing required fields, compatible sequences, cell counts, or closure policy can break old DTBs even though this source file itself has no runtime storage.

**Dependencies and integration points:** Linux thermal sensor drivers match `compatible`, map sensor registers, acquire clocks/resets/interrupts/nvmem calibration, and register thermal-sensor providers for `thermal-zones`. Integration is with `thermal-sensor.yaml`, trip-point policy in board DTS, cooling-device governors, SoC calibration cells, and interrupt/polling paths for over-temperature events. Schema-level dependencies are the core Devicetree meta-schema plus none declared.

**Risks:** Main risks are Devicetree ABI drift, incomplete compatible coverage, stale examples, wrong required-property sets, mismatched register-window order, and conditionals that are either too broad or too strict for existing board DTS files. Thermal-specific risk centers on missing calibration nvmem cells, wrong sensor cell counts, omitted clocks/resets/interrupts, SoC variant conditionals, and trip-zone wiring that can leave thermal protection inactive or report incorrect temperatures. Because these schemas are part of the DT ABI, tightening closure or changing compatible ordering should be treated as a compatibility-sensitive change.

**Test signals:** Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/thermal/qcom-lmh.yaml` and `make dtbs_check` for DTS users of every compatible listed here. Verify `$ref` targets resolve, inline examples compile, required-property failures are caught, and compatible-specific `if`/`then` branches are exercised.  Runtime signals include thermal zone registration, plausible sensor readings, nvmem calibration reads, trip interrupts or polling behavior, and cooling-device engagement under controlled temperature tests.
